ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U2	Let's move on to today's agenda. Agenda item 16: Second reading of the draft law amending the Waste Act and the Packaging Act initiated by the Government of the Republic. I give the floor to Yoko Alender, Chairman of the Committee on the Environment.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U3	[[Remote connection]] Ladies and gentlemen, Good manager. Nice new work date! We have before us the draft law amending the Waste Act and the Packaging Act at second reading. The draft law was initiated by the Government of the Republic on 11 May 2020. I recall that the minister who introduced the draft to us at first reading was Rene Chef. The draft amends the requirements and measures relating to the separate collection of waste, extended producer responsibility, prevention of domestic waste, the improvement of municipal waste management plans and the recycling of waste in order to achieve better results in the preparation and recycling of waste re-use. The wording of the liability provisions and the penalty rates are also specified. The first reading of the draft was completed on 20 May 2020 and the amendments were tabled by 3 June. The member of the Riigikogu Mart Võrguship tabled three amendments; the other members of the Riigikogu, the committees and the political groups did not propose amendments to the draft. Mart Võrguship withdrew its proposals on the first and third March. The Estonian Pandipakend, the Association of Estonian Cities and Municipalities, the Estonian Chamber of Commerce and Industry, the Association of Estonian Circular Economy Enterprises, the Estonian Chamber of Agricultural Commerce, the residents of Narva City and the Ministry of the Environment have also submitted their draft opinion and proposals for amending the draft. The Commission discussed the draft for the preparation of the second reading on 13 July and 8 December 2020 and on 6 and 8 April that year. On 13 July last year a very wide range of interest groups was involved and various proposals were presented. On 8 December only representatives of the Ministry took part and 15 of the amendments tabled were voted on. These were technical and linguistic improvements to the draft. After the change of government, we decided to continue to deal with the bill. On 6 April, we again invited a relatively wide range of interest groups to the Commission: Estonian Re-use Organisation, Estonian Packaging Circular, Producer Responsibility Organisation, Association of Circular Economy Enterprises, Association of Cities and Municipalities, Chamber of Commerce, Trade Union, Association of Drinks Producers, Food Industry Association, Chemical Industry Association and Ministry of the Environment. At the meeting of the Commission, the stakeholders presented their proposals. The main topics were the organisation of local collection of packaging waste and the need to clean up the surrounding area of public packaging containers. We also held a debate on the method of sampling waste, and it was also a question of the levels of penalties. The audit of packaging companies was also discussed, which is also the subject of the draft. At the meeting, it was clarified that the local government unit could organise separate collections of packaging waste from the place of origin, in agreement with the recycling organisation referred to in §101 (1) of the Packaging Act, and the recycling organisation would then be guaranteed participation in the management of the recovery of packaging waste in proportion to its market share. This means that recycling organisations will be given access to recovered packaging waste. It was considered important to point out that the local government unit cooperates with all recovery organisations, not just one of them. In the past, the local government unit has also had the right to include the collection of packaging waste in the organised waste shipment, but unfortunately this option has not been widely used. This right was previously derived from Paragraph 66 (2) of the Waste Act and pursuant to that paragraph, the local government unit can arrange for the collection of packaging waste from the site without agreement with the recycling organisation, but in this case the collection of packaging waste will also be maintained in parallel through public collection facilities operated by the organisations. And in this case, joining the service is voluntary for the holder of the waste, or people. Now, in conjunction with this previous section, local authorities are given the opportunity to make the collection of packaging waste from the source mandatory for waste holders, but only on condition that there is cooperation with recycling organisations and that the service price is less than EUR 30 cents. Otherwise, membership is optional. The amendment will help reduce the quantity of packaging waste in the composition of mixed municipal waste and better quality packaging waste will reach recovery organisations. And if the local government unit... agreement with the recycling organisation to collect from the site of origin, then with the agreement of the local government body, the recycling organisation may reduce the number and volume of these public containers in proportion to the number of participants in the collection from the place of origin of packaging waste. [[The connection's breaking up.]] The draft specifies how the packaging could still be collected at the preferred site of origin. Representatives of the Ministry of the Environment also took part in the meeting on 8 April and a position was drawn up on the Mart Võrguship's amendment, which was still there. The Commission also decided to propose four Commission amendments. A little of the amendments, then. Amendments 1 and 8 are related and we have Mart Võrguship's proposal on list 8. The member of the Riigikogu proposes to exclude from the planned Waste Act and Packaging Act Amendment Act § 1 96, which supplements the Waste Act and gives the Environmental Inspectorate the right to exercise administrative supervision over the administrative acts of the local government. The author of the amendment considers that, since it is a review of the legality of administrative acts and not of the content, it is not justified to entrust these tasks to the Environmental Board. The Ministry of Justice already has the competence and competence to monitor the legality of administrative acts. This amendment was fully taken into account by the Commission and relates to Amendment 1. Amendments 4, 14, 15, 19 and 20 are on the Commission's side and on the basis of ministerial proposals on the same subject. Amendment 14 is perhaps the most substantive, it is not technical. This is a new amendment which gives the municipality the right to organise the handling of containers of overfilled public packaging waste and packaging waste gathered around it. Before the local authority clears the containers or disposes of the packaging waste, it must allow the recycling organisation to do so itself. To this end, the local authority must inform the recycling organisation in writing and set a reasonable time to empty the container. It should also be fixed in an agreement between the parties. It is also important for the agreement to reflect the conditions under which the municipality itself organises the shipment of packaging waste and invoices it to the recycling organisation. It is important that the recycling organisation is given the opportunity to empty the filled container or to dispose of packaging waste stored next to the container. Stakeholders also expressed the view that this agreement between themselves is very important and in practice it often happens that way. But there are still problems. The flexibility of the emptying schedules is often insufficient, and the aim of this provision is to provide a clearer solution for ensuring the welfare of the public container park. Amendments 19 and 20 relate to the fact that the draft did not enter into force as a law in 2020, i.e. the deadlines. Amendment 20 amends the date of entry into force in 2021 by 1 January 2022. The addresses will then have sufficient time to examine the amendments after the entry into force of the draft law and to take account of the new requirements. The references to paragraphs 5 and 6 of § 2 are deleted from the text of § 3 of the draft act, as they enter into force in the general procedure. Amendment 7 concerns the audit of the right to finance the obligations laid down in the principle of extended producer responsibility. It has been drafted as an amendment by the Commission on the proposal of the Audit Committee. This provision has been clarified for reasons of legal clarity and the task is to carry out a check on whether sufficient funds are available to finance the above commitments. Amendment 16 is a linguistic amendment proposed by the Estonian Pandipakend. One word has been omitted from the wording. Amendments 2, 3, 5, 6, 9 to 13, 17 and 18 are of a technical nature on the part of the Commission and are subject to the relevant provisions of the Rules on Standard Techniques for Drafts to be dealt with in the Riigikogu. The Commission also took procedural decisions. It was decided to send the draft for second reading on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu by 14 April. When the second reading of the draft is completed, the draft will be placed on the plenary agenda of the Riigikogu for the third reading on 21 April. And I'd be happy to answer your questions. Thank you.

I give the floor for the presentation to Minister for Social Protection Signe Riisalo. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U49	[[Remote connection]] Good morning. Thank you very much, President of the Riigikogu. Honourable ambassadors! I will try to be constructive at this time of day and bring to you three amendments to the Family Benefits Act. The first change is different parental benefit for parents who are unemployed under the COVID conditions. It shall have a fixed term. The other two are designed to last. One is the payment of family benefits to the parents of a stillborn child and the other is the payment of family allowances to young people aged 16 to 19 who are studying at the beginning of the academic year. But now more specifically. First of all, the part of the amendment of the Family Benefits Act concerning the calculation of the amount of parental benefit in the event of unemployment. Indeed, the labour market situation already last spring and the country as a whole created a number of unemployed people for us. Today, unemployment has increased to about 8.8%. It changes every week, and the last time I looked at it, I saw a number just like that. In order to avoid a delay in receiving children or a deterioration in the livelihood of the beneficiary of parental benefit who has lost his or her job due to the corona crisis, a change is planned whereby the period of unemployment is excluded from the period for calculating parental benefit. We have calculated that 80% of births are 23 to 37 years old, and we have calculated how much potential there could be of birthers who have become unemployed because of the situation caused by coronavir. We have come to the conclusion that the measure could affect 380 women and 80 men. The cost of such a so-called customer base is EUR 2.9 million per year. Given the size of the budget for parental benefits in the state budget, it should be noted that this expenditure is less than 1% of the total budget. The fact is also that such a change requires the cost of developing the IT system. This expenditure is planned from the Ministry of Social Affairs' own resources and amounts to EUR 63000. This part of the draft is planned to enter into force on 1 July inclusive, but it is also proposed that it should be implemented retroactively from 1 January inclusive. This is precisely with the aim that, in a positive sense, parents who became unemployed last spring during the period of the previous emergency, would also be affected during the period of increased disease spread. The second amendment is the payment of family benefits to the parents of a stillborn child. First of all, in order to prevent a question, I'll tell you who we consider to be a stillborn child. These are stillborn children of at least 500 grams birth weight and after 22 weeks of pregnancy. We have had children like this in Estonia in the last few years 40 to 60 years, but this number has been decreasing all the time. In 2019 we had 28 such children. Estimates in terms of state budget expenditure have been made on a small margin based not on 2019 statistics, but on 40 potential stillborn children per year. This would mean an additional cost to the state budget of EUR 13000 per year. What is the content of this amendment? What do we offer to parents who have suffered a great loss? Fathers can take paternity leave and receive an additional parental benefit of 30 calendar days from the birth of the child. Similarly, mothers who are not entitled to maternity benefit under the Health Insurance Act are entitled to parental benefit. Since this target group is very small, but still extremely vulnerable due to the loss of the body, obviously we can all imagine how deep the grief is in the event of the loss of a child, we must also take into account the fact that mothers are able to restore their health. So the kind of support that has started at the initiative of the target group itself and also found support from the Ministry of Social Affairs and also......... support. [[The connection's breaking up.]] This is definitely our task as a caring society. The third amendment is of a very practical nature. Up to 16 years of age and 16 to 19 years of age, children's allowance is paid if they continue their studies. We have 535 children in the year who do not continue their studies, and their children are entitled to one month's child benefit according to the draft. The reason is that the Education Information System (EHIS) collects data and transmits them to the Social Insurance Board with a small stay, but since the Social Insurance Board pays out child benefits at the beginning of September, the data is not complete by that time. The current practice has left families in a situation where, between one month and the other, family benefits are not paid retroactively. In order to ensure a stable family income also in the form of child benefits, our proposal is to extend it by one month. And then, indeed, 535 children will receive one month's support as a result, while the rest will receive support simply without this one-month break. Of course, it is paid retroactively now. This cost to the state budget is EUR 60000 per year. And the development cost of the SKAIS is an additional EUR 50000 one-off cost. This is briefly the content of these amendments. I'm ready to answer the vigilantes' questions.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U64	Thank you. The draft, which deals with the amendment of the Family Benefits Act and bears the number 300, was initiated by Riina Solman, Minister of Population in the Government of Jüri Ratas, and this was the first discussion we had in the Committee on Social Affairs on 12 January when the Minister and his good assistants, the advisers, presented this draft. Since Minister Signe Riisalo has already spoken quite a bit about its content, I am not going to repeat it. But as we were saying, the basic idea is that it would help people who are now working for some reason during the corona virus and are having a baby. This period, when they are unemployed, does not go into the parental benefit calculation and may not delay the adoption of children for this reason. The problem committee on the resolution of the population crisis sent us one proposal and, on the proposal of Tõnis Möldri, the chairman of the then Commission, we had to discuss it between the first and second readings. This is yet to be done by the Commission. Now the questions and the debate that took place in the Commission. The good Commissioner, Helmen Hunter, had a question. Tta thought it was a good and noble bill, but thought it would be too late before Parliament, could have come much earlier. His question was how this draft concerns women working in different forms of work, such as women who are self-employed or who work under a contract of command. The minister today just said that it concerns everyone. But then Merlin Murumets of the Ministry told us that the amendment was indeed intended to be made earlier, when the amendment of the Family Benefits Act extended the period of protection of successive births. But then it failed to do so because it required more in-depth analysis and IT changes. The planned changes will benefit all unemployed people who remain pregnant when these changes are in force. Member of the Commission Signe Riisalo examined whether it was reasonable to do so through a parental benefit scheme or whether it would have been reasonable to find a solution through labour market measures. The answer was that this draft had two main objectives: firstly, to support families wishing to have a child, and secondly, to ensure that there is no delay in having children. Consequently, it was designed as a change to the parental benefit scheme. It was a question that, if the purpose of this draft is that children should not be born as a result of the crisis and those who have lost their jobs due to the crisis would receive help, would this law not discriminate against women who do not work on the basis of an employment contract but are self-employed? And the question is also that a certain group of young women will be deprived of support. It was Mrs. Helmen Hunt's practical proofs. The representative of the Ministry explained that self-employed persons who have lost their jobs can also register at the unemployment fund. If he has paid the tax correctly, the period of his unemployment is also covered. So such individuals will benefit from the planned change. Then there was the question of what would happen to those women who left work in the spring and did not register as unemployed or took unpaid leave during an emergency, as their home-schooled children needed support at home and therefore were unable to actively seek work. The Ministry's advisor replied that the draft did not cover the target group whose salary had been reduced or those who had been protected by the wage subsidy measure. If a woman received a salary allowance, this period will be included in the calculation of the parental benefit because it was subject to social tax. It was stressed that the draft supports those who lost their pay and jobs. The aim is to protect the most vulnerable target group. Women whose wages have been reduced lose in parental benefits, but their loss is not as drastic as those who did not have a salary at all. It should be said that, of course, this was also the most important issue in our four debates in the Commission. And before I actually finish, I'm sure I'm gonna stop by on this one more time. It was then proposed that this bill be brought to this large hall on 20 January. This proposal was supported by consensus. That I could report the draft was also supported by consensus. But then something happened. The first meeting of the Commission we discussed was on 12 January. Perhaps a good chairman and other colleagues will remember that then came a more difficult day, when the papers, for example, said that Mikhail Korb made it back from Azerbaijan by train. And there were some other important events. And when the committee reunited on 18 January, the chairman, Tõnis Mölder, proposed that this draft be withdrawn from the agenda of the large Chamber. Me and my colleague, Helmen Kütt, did not support this because we felt that the draft was urgent, we should proceed. But a good colleague, Tõnis Mölder, pointed out that since there may be a problem with the fact that there is no belief that it deals with all women equally, it still needs to be examined and discussed. But I mean, the real reason was something else, because very soon three people left the Commission for different ministries, there was a change of government. And then it was really decided that this draft would be withdrawn from the plenary agenda. It was supported by 6 delegates and 3 opposed. For the third time, we discussed this draft on 5 April. Then Signe Riisalo introduced the bill, just like today. As has been said, in addition to the aforementioned idea of parental benefit measures, there were two aspects: the amendment of the Family Benefits Act and support for the parents of stillborn children. As a result, there were questions in the Commission. The question was why this IT solution cost is very high. It was explained to us that since this system is automatic, it will take time to reschedule. But this cost of EUR 50 000 for the draft itself seemed disproportionate to us, to the Members of the Commission. There was also the question of the criteria for the concept of a stillborn child. As the Minister said today, there are two criteria: at least 22 weeks of pregnancy and the child weighs at least 500 grams. There was also the question of whether both conditions had to be met. The answer was that the most important thing is this 22 weeks of pregnancy. Before that, it's a miscarriage, not a stillbirth. Then there was the question of how stillborn children are registered in Estonia. In response, we received a death note and a death certificate for children, which are not included in the population register. But they will be given a unique personal identification number, which will remain on the medical system and on the cause of death record. It was also pointed out that it would be possible to register stillborn children in the population register, give their children a name and personal identification code. If parents so wanted, it would probably help in some cases to overcome grief more easily. The question was asked that, in the current crisis situation, not only women who have become unemployed but also those whose workload has been reduced or who have to go on unpaid leave for a period of time. The new chairman of the committee, Siret Kotka, asked whether the parental benefit measures set out in Bill 300 necessarily required registration as unemployed and how this would increase the compensation received. In response, we have found that the target group is women who have lost their jobs due to the crisis, and it does not extend to other target groups. It is important for the country to help all those in need, but this is not always possible and therefore the most affected group was chosen. The reduced income does not have as much impact on parental benefits as the lack of pay. In the same way as in the first sitting of the Commission, part of the members of the committee have come to the question of whether it is more of a labour market or a family benefit measure. As I said before, the officials still agreed that this was a family benefit measure. Issues related to the self-employed also arose in the same way. Here Mr Pohlak was wondering if there would be an extension of the measure if the corona crisis were to be prolonged. Minister Riisalo said that this was a temporary measure and that it would be analysed. As he replied here today, it is not certain that it makes sense to continue as a family benefit measure, perhaps it still needs to be transformed into a labour market measure. There was again a debate on women who are self-employed, such as many beauty service providers, etc., whose work on the closure of shopping centres is clearly limited and who are not responsible for it. They have not registered as unemployed either, because their activities are not specifically restricted. Their activities are indirectly limited, as people cannot go to shopping centres and use their services. But as we were saying, these people are not concerned with the bill, it only concerns those who have officially registered as unemployed. We have spoken with the chairman of the committee, other members of the committee and officials that this is probably an important nuance on this draft, which we will certainly, I dare say, before we discuss the second reading. I will present at second reading what the Commission's final position is, how we deal with the various forms of work. Sometimes wage cuts or unpaid leave actually have a greater or at least equal effect on people's incomes than unemployment. Perhaps the law allows for equal consideration of these different nuances. This debate is probably still ahead of us. But on 5 April, the decision was made again to send this draft to the Grand Hall and to propose that the first reading be completed. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U99	Thank you. First of all, I will start at the back end, yes, this Baltic bubble, or the lightening border crossing between the Baltic States, was in force until 5 April, when we completed the procedure that the previous government had put in place. This means that we have common rules on border crossings with our neighbours. For example, in the case of Latvia, if a person is not a resident of Valga or Valka, he or she is subject to the same requirement of self-insulation or testing as those arriving from other Schengen areas. This means that border crossingrs must remain in isolation for 10 days, which can be released by means of two tests: the first test either 72 hours before coming to the country or on arrival and the second one not earlier than the sixth day after the first test was carried out. There are also exceptions that apply to the passenger service provider, to the carriers, as well as to those who have been vaccinated and those who have suffered from it. So it's the order that's in place right now. The question about the airport. At the airport, people should fill in a health form and then have the opportunity, as described above, to take the opportunity to take two tests or to be isolated. The point of filling in this health form is to know exactly where people are located, because then you can check whether they are fulfilling their isolation obligation. Testing cannot be made mandatory for the citizens of the Republic of Estonia. We have a constitution that talks about physical integrity, the Supreme Court has furnished it with its judgment, and no one can violently be forced to take the test. If you don't take the test, you'll have an isolation obligation, as I said before, just to prevent the infection from spreading and the infection from being brought into the country. We have also allocated additional resources, or at least we have talked in the government office about the need for more police officers to check border crossings and health insurance at the airport. So maybe it should work better now if it worked a few weeks ago.

If we look at the indicators of absolute poverty, in fact 2.3% of the total Estonian population is in absolute poverty and only 1% of them are of retirement age. The situation with relative poverty is much more complicated, with 41.6% of people over the age of 65 in relative poverty. The picture becomes even more complex when it comes to a single-living pensioner, because 76.8% of them are in relative poverty. Of course, the biggest concern of the current government is how to maintain and increase pensionable incomes. A stable environment, including legislation, is certainly what helps to maintain a person's faith in the pension pool itself. On the other hand, a stable increase in pensions is also a challenge in order to meet people's real needs and costs. This coalition has set two goals. First of all, we plan to make extraordinary increases in old age pensions. I am in the Ministry of Social Affairs preparing a bill for changing both the basic pension and the national pension rate. However, the Ministry of Finance is preparing a draft, which should also include a tax exemption for the average old-age pension. So these are the plans of this government alongside teaching people about saving, collecting, and also smart choices with the second pillar.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U213	Honourable Vice-Chairman of the Riigikogu! Colleagues! Dear Minister, It is no secret that the Estonian state has been spending longer than its powers, and that was also the case before the Coronavirus crisis. The State's costs exceeded revenue even when the economy was still in good times. At the time of the adoption of this year's state budget, the government forecast the general government budget deficit at around 7% of GDP. According to the new coalition agreement of the government led by Kaja Kallas, the government's aim is to quote'move towards a balance with the state budget', while under the coalition agreement, the government will again quote 'don't introduce new taxes and ensure a stable tax environment'. If there is no substantial increase in the State's tax revenue, the State must therefore reduce its expenditure. Prime Minister Kaja Kallas said to the national broadcasters last Sunday that his government (I quote again) "must find places to kill and make painful decisions." Several economic experts, as well as international institutions such as the OECD, have strongly advised Estonia to consider changes in the tax structure, including the introduction of property taxes. Estonia's tax policy favours the wealthiest people in many ways. One example is that in the previous crisis, VAT was raised to increase the revenue of the state, which, of course, hits more households with lower incomes. We don't tax property. For several decades, we have not carried out a regular land assessment. As a result, for example, the collection of land tax constitutes a rather insignificant part of the budget of the city of Tallinn, the largest municipality of Estonia. Estonia is known to be the only country in Europe where cars are not taxed, and we are the last country in the European Union in terms of the share of property taxes in GDP. When proposals for tax changes have been made, they are generally immediately rejected. I would remind you that the Minister for Social Affairs of the previous government proposed a solution for financing long-term care through care insurance. It didn't get anywhere. The city of Tallinn has proposed the introduction of a tourism tax and the creation of an opportunity to generate additional revenue for the city. That hasn't got anywhere either. The Minister for Health of the current government has proposed one idea on how to provide additional funding for health care, and this was also rejected without further ado by the other party of the government coalition. At the same time, for example, the United States and the United Kingdom, which have also increased expenditure and budget deficits in this crisis, are considering very seriously and are planning to make various tax changes to finance this area in the future. In its annual report submitted to the Riigikogu last year, the National Audit Office has stated that the sustainable offer of priority services, education, medical care, rescue and police is, in fact, in question everywhere in Estonia except Harjumaa and Tartumaa. And these are major systemic problems that require longer reflection than one electoral cycle. Certainly, the introduction or consideration of property taxes would not solve these concerns quickly, would not solve all these problems, but the debate on property taxes is still an opportunity to move forward with a broader debate on taxes. And I believe that it is precisely for this reason that Mr Hansson, the economic adviser to Kaja Kallas, raised this issue. I'm sure Stenbock's house will never do anything by accident. Finally. It is absolutely peculiar that we have taken a position in an exceptionally rapid state of technological, economic and social change, and for years and years and years, in a very long period of time, that changing the tax system is a complete taboo issue. Most Estonian political parties and politicians want to address the issue of taxation as much as the sea mine that has come to the surface. I think that there is an exceptionally welcome expertise in handling both taxes and mines, as well as a calm mind. And that is what I would like for the debate that follows here.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U270	Mr President, I would like to thank you very much. Minister, thank you very much. Good Riigikogu! Honourable listeners! We are now discussing an issue that is a very important matter of the value of Estonian foreign policy. This concerns Estonia's relations with the People's Republic of China. As a nation state founded on the principle of self-determination of peoples, Estonia is in a very sensitive situation whenever it is a question of following these principles at international level. And that is why on 22 February we have presented an inquiry to the Foreign Minister, which, as an important point in the discussion of foreign policy before the Riigikogu on 16 February, highlights the fact that Estonia is extremely important in its good relations with China, that Estonia recognises a Chinese policy and that there is no intention to change it. The Minister of Foreign Affairs also named Taiwan as one of the Chinese regions. We believe that this is contrary to the current external policy development plan, which sets among other things objectives the preservation of a world order based on international law, serious consequences for violations of international law and the protection of fundamental rights and freedoms. It also requires Estonia to consistently focus on violations of international law by referring to specific perpetrators, to support countries that are politically and materially victims of aggression, to stand up for the consistent application of democracy and the rule of law, to stand up for freedom of religion and belief and the protection of religious minorities in the world, to uphold the rights of indigenous peoples, including the right to self-determination of peoples and to support the preservation and development of the cultural and linguistic diversity of the world. The Beijing regime, which controls most of China's historic territory, has systematically violated human rights and fundamental freedoms, international law and the rights of indigenous peoples in consolidating and expanding its power. Examples of this include the annexation of Tibet in 1951, the ongoing suppression of peaceful resistance, the restriction of religious freedom, the imprisonment of religious leaders and the policy of renationalisation, the imprisonment of the civilian population of Uighur in concentration camps in Xinjiang, their violent treatment, the obstruction of the Uighurs' right to self-determination, which is also increasingly called at international level genocide, the obstruction of the rights of indigenous people of internal Mongolia and the suppression of peaceful resistance, the threat of military invasion by the government of the legitimate Republic of China in Taiwan and repeated military provocations, the brutal violation of international commitments undertaken in guaranteeing the rights of Hong Kong and the suppression of this region to central government. Unfortunately, the views expressed by the Minister of Foreign Affairs, especially by referring to one of the Chinese policies, allow Estonia's position to be interpreted as a statement of support for the activities of the People's Republic of China in establishing its power over indigenous peoples and also to refrain from defending the right of the indigenous peoples to self-determination. It is clear that such a step would be contrary to the objectives set out in the foreign policy development plan and would seriously call into question the value-based nature of Estonian foreign policy. We therefore, as heirs, expect the Foreign Minister to answer eight questions that I am not going to read in the face of a lack of time. Thank you.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U271	The foreign minister Eva-Maria Liimets will answer the question. Please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U272	[[Remote connection]] Good afternoon. I hope I'm audible and visible.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U273	Fine.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U274	Thank you. Honourable Members of the Riigikogu! Mr Kaalep, thank you very much for raising these questions and the issue and the problem. I'll answer your eight questions first. The promotion and protection of human rights remains the cornerstone and priority of the external action of Estonia and the European Union. We are constantly working to ensure that serious violations of human rights are not overlooked and condemned. When assessing the human rights situation in different countries, we are always based on a number of sources. In the case of the People's Republic of China, the information we receive from the Estonian embassy in Beijing is important, where the analysis of what is happening in the country of residence is carried out on a daily basis and information about all current issues is constantly transmitted. We also have access, as a Member State of the European Union, to information provided by the Union's Representation in Beijing. In addition, we have overviews of discussions in the international organisations of which Estonia is a member, such as the UN and its sub-organisations, the UN Committee for the Monitoring of International Conventions and reports by independent international institutions. Estonian diplomats with the UN and other international organisations in Geneva also meet regularly with national minority organisations and associations. Based on the diverse sources, we must say that the information available on the situation of indigenous peoples in China is a serious concern. We have reason to believe that freedom of religion and belief and the rights of minorities are being restricted. We have therefore repeatedly raised this issue and made critical statements in various formats calling for changes to the situation in China. The second question and the answer to that. Since the restoration of independence, Estonia has been guided by a Chinese policy. The diplomatic relations of the Republic of Estonia were concluded with the People's Republic of China. This means that the Republic of Estonia treats the People's Republic of China as a subject of international law. Without violating this principle, Estonia, as well as the European Union as a whole, communicates in non-political areas such as trade, investment, education and cultural cooperation, such as Taiwan. Estonia has supported Taiwan's participation in the World Health Assembly, together with other countries of the same kind, which is particularly important given the need to combat the global COVID-19 pandemic. One Chinese policy has no influence on the implementation of our foreign policy development plan. Answer to question three. In recent years, Estonia has consistently raised questions about the human rights situation in all possible formats, both in our bilateral relations with China, at European Union level and with other co-ordinate countries in international organisations. Estonia has not been left out of any critical statement on the situation in China in recent years under the UN. The statements have criticised arbitrary detentions, large-scale surveillance and other restrictions on human rights, in particular with regard to Uighurs and other minorities in Xinjiang. Hong Kong's security and legislation issues have also been raised several times. For example, in July 2019 Estonia joined the United Nations Human Rights Council in a joint letter of 21 countries on the human rights situation in Xinjiang. In June 2020, Estonia joined the 28 countries' joint statement at the UN Human Rights Council, which was about the human rights situation in Xinjiang and Hong Kong. On Xinjiang Estonia also joined the United Nations General Assembly's Third Committee in 2019 with a joint declaration by 23 countries and in 2020 with a joint declaration by 39 countries, which in addition to Xinjiang also dealt with Hong Kong. We have also spoken about China in the UN Security Council, of which Estonia is currently a non-permanent member. For example, in May 2020, Estonia raised the issue of legislation threatening Hong Kong's autonomy in conjunction with High Representative Borrell, Germany and the United Kingdom at a meeting on cooperation between the European Union and the United Nations. In May 2020, Hong Kong held a discussion during the Estonian Presidency of the UN Security Council. Estonia has joined the International Media Freedom Coalition and the International Alliance for Freedom of Religion and Persuading, which has also discussed and made statements about human rights violations in China. For example, in January 2021, 16 members of the Media Freedom Coalition, including Estonia, made a statement on restrictions on media freedom, also pointing out that the journalist Chen Jieren was a prisoner for writing about COVID-19. In November 2018, in the framework of the UN General Periodic Review of Human Rights, the so-called UPR, Estonia made a number of recommendations to China to improve the human rights situation, such as the Covenant on Civil and Political Rights (ICCPR) and its Additional Protocol on the abolition of the death penalty, the Additional Protocol to the Convention against Torture and the accession to the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court. Estonia also called on China to ensure free access to the Internet, guarantee freedom of expression and ban the physical punishment of children. In December 2018, Estonia, together with other like - minded countries, issued a statement condemning China's massive theft of intellectual property in cyberspace. In the European Union, Estonia has supported statements on human rights in Hong Kong and has actively contributed to shaping common positions. We have worked for a long time to get the sanction regime against human rights offenders adopted by the European Union around the world. For the first time, the European Union has created a framework for targeted action against individuals, entities and bodies, including national and non-state actors responsible for serious violations of human rights worldwide, participating in, or associated with them, regardless of where they were committed. These restrictive measures provide for a travel ban on individuals and a freezing of funds both for individuals and for entities. In addition, persons and entities in the European Union shall be prohibited from making funds available directly or indirectly to designated persons, entities or bodies. The framework for targeted restrictive measures applies to acts such as genocide, crimes against humanity and other serious violations of human rights, such as torture, slavery, out-of-court executions, demonstrations, arrests and detentions. Other human rights violations may also fall within the scope of the sanctioning regime if they are comprehensive, systematic or otherwise difficult for the objectives of the common foreign and security policy as set out in the Treaty. The establishment, review and amendment of the list of sanctions shall be the responsibility of the Council acting on a proposal from a Member State or the High Representative of the European Union for Foreign Affairs and Security Policy. Thus, the new human rights sanction regime in the European Union brings additional opportunities for sanctioning human rights offenders. We believe that this instrument must be used, if necessary, against specific perpetrators of human rights violations. On the bilateral side, we have raised human rights and the situation in Hong Kong at almost all meetings with Chinese colleagues. I also stressed the need for respect for human rights in my speech at the recent summit with China and the countries of Central and Eastern Europe. It is important to know that imposing sanctions is a legal issue. The attribution of human rights violations is always subject to specific evidence and an independent international investigation is often required to collect them. Unfortunately, in two years, UN High Commissioner for Human Rights Michelle Bachelet has not given the opportunity to get to know the situation on the ground in Xinjiang, nor has the European Union received this opportunity. The visit by Xinjiang to the ambassadors of the Member States of the European Union in Beijing has not taken place on the grounds that meetings with the people wanted by the European Union are not guaranteed. Answer to Question 4. The Chinese security law enforced in Hong Kong and its arrests and the removal of elected ambassadors are contrary to the principle of "one country, two systems" and the international commitments made by China. Estonia continues to draw attention to violations of international law in international organisations, the European Union and bilaterally. Since China refused to accept the European Union Member States' demarš on Hong Kong in Beijing, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs did demarš in Tallinn in the same way as other European Union Member States. Estonia prefers to continue to act with its allies on such matters. Answer to Question 5. We are based on the principle that threats of force or use of force are not acceptable and do not comply with the UN Charter. It is important for us that the actions of the Member States of the European Union and NATO's allies on these issues should be coherent. Answer to Question 6. It has always been our view that human rights are universal and that their fulfilment is not just an internal matter for any country. One Chinese policy does not exempt countries from their obligations to comply with international law and to ensure compliance with international obligations under the UN Charter and international agreements to which the People's Republic of China is a party. Estonia has consistently pointed out violations of international law and continues to do so. Answer to Question 7. Estonia has not missed any suitable opportunity to draw attention to these problems. We have consistently made joint statements with other countries that require respect for human rights, including in Xinjiang, and supported the European Union's speeches on this issue in UN sub-organisations. For example, in April 2019 the European Union called for the release of Uighur detainees in addition to other human rights defenders at the EU-China Human Rights Dialogue. Answer to Question 8. We have addressed this issue at the meeting of the International Alliance for Freedom of Religion and Persuasion, which discussed the issue of freedom of religion in Tibet. Both the Tibetan Support Group of the Riigikogu, the Estonian Institute of Buddhism and other associations and organisations have been calling for the release of political prisoners and the cessation of violence in Tibet for years. In April 2020, 32 Members of the European Parliament called for the unconditional release of the Pantchen Lama XI. In April 2019, the European Union called for the release of Tibetan activists, writers and religious figures, including Tashi Wangchuki and Lobsang Dargye, at the Human Rights Dialogue meeting with China. This was a compact overview of what Estonia is doing in its foreign policy to urge the People's Republic of China to respect human rights, given the universality of human rights. Thanks for asking me about this. I'll finish here.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U326	Thank you, good vice chairman. Good coworkers! Good people who are watching us over the Internet! This is what we have in real life: our employees, our ministers, are talking about one thing, but we as employers and when representatives of the people live their lives here. And I am honoured to read a document that I myself signed as chairman of the Hong Kong Support Group of the Riigikogu. The influence of the People's Republic of China threatens academic freedoms and freedom of the press. Public address to the Riigikogu, the Government of the Republic, the governing bodies of Estonian universities, independent research institutions, journalism publications and all scientists, academics, journalists, cultural people and politicians working in Estonia. The increasing and expanding influence of the People's Republic of China on a global scale, targeting both academic studies in China and the free press in China, has become obvious and has received widespread media responses and monitoring reports. The authorities of the People's Republic of China have targeted investigators, journalists and institutions that are considered hostile to China. In particular, this concerns sensitive issues for China, such as some assessments of genocide-related repressions in Tibet and East Turkestan, restrictions on religious freedom throughout the country, disregarding the rule of law in Hong Kong and so on. This involves monitoring and tracking researchers and journalists, direct intimidation, sanctions, expulsion from China and the dissemination of visa languages, false information, as well as lure and bribery. Let us draw attention to some recent incidents that have led to public discussion: sanctions against ten politicians and investigators in the European Union in the People's Republic of China and four institutions which include a ban on entry into the People's Republic of China, including Hong Kong and Macau, and a ban on business, which also extends to persons and companies associated with them. Secondly, the pursuit and obstruction of journalists working in China and the expulsion from the country. During the last year, at least 18 foreign journalists have been found to be expelled from China. Thirdly, a recent commitment at the University of Oxford in the United Kingdom to provide students with their research work on Chinese research anonymously in order to protect students from Hong Kong, in particular, from possible repression by the authorities of the People's Republic of China, which gives them the opportunity of the so-called Hong Kong National Security Act established last year. On the basis of the above, we consider that Estonia cannot and must not remain in a passive and predictable position regarding the increasing pressure and influence of the People's Republic of China, as it is small countries that are most vulnerable to such influence. Estonia must establish and ensure with national policies (in cooperation with the European Union and other countries) the ability to monitor the influence of the People's Republic of China on an ongoing basis and, if necessary, to implement rapid countermeasures, to protect the security of its citizens as well as the students of foreigners studying and working here, especially China, Hong Kong and Macau, and to guarantee academic freedoms for all in the field of Chinese research. To this end, we call for: all Estonian students, researchers and lecturers as well as the leaders of universities and research institutions conduct and organise scientific research and academic discussions on Chinese topics freely, without censorship and self-censorship, and without allowing the authorities of the People's Republic of China to influence research in any public or hidden way in a direction that could tilt the results of the investigation and, more broadly, limit academic freedoms; all Estonian journalists and media publications be attentive and responsive to official and informal attempts by the People's Republic of China and demand and ensure access to all necessary sources of information concerning China; The Government of the Republic will invest significantly more and more intensively in Chinese research to create a better and more comprehensive capacity to understand China and disseminate objective information related to China, including strengthening cooperation between government agencies and researchers; The Riigikogu, in cooperation with the Government of the Republic, universities, research institutions, think tanks, experts, will develop a comprehensive and up-to-date Chinese policy that will provide a legal framework for responding quickly and effectively to Chinese influence...

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U335	[[Remote connection]] Thank you, honourable Members of the Riigikogu, for these questions. We are pleased to continue this debate and to respond to the five questions that we have just heard. The Kingdom of Spain is an important partner for the Republic of Estonia in the European Union and a close ally in NATO. The bilateral relations between Estonia and Spain are strong and long-term, and on 8 March we celebrated the 100th anniversary of diplomatic relations. Spain is a democratic rule of law, so there is no doubt that its internal political disputes will be resolved democratically in accordance with existing legislation and the Spanish Constitution. Estonia supports the sovereignty and territorial integrity of Spain. Catalonia is an autonomous region of a democratic state and all processes for seeking greater autonomy must be carried out democratically and in compliance with Spanish law, in compliance with the rule of law, and in accordance with the Spanish constitutional process. The removal of the immunity of Members of the European Parliament, Carles Puigdemont, Anton Comin and Clara Ponsat, was a decision taken by the European Parliament during the democratic voting process, which was also in line with the assessment made by the European Parliament's Committee on Legal Affairs. The Member States of the European Union and the other institutions of the European Union have no competence to intervene in the European Parliament's decision-making process. In accordance with the Treaties on European Union, the European Commission controls the enforcement of European Union law, including the Treaties, by the Member States. The Treaties of the European Union provide for appropriate procedures for this purpose. The right of people to self-determination is a very important principle of international law. The main content of the right to self-determination is the right of a national minority to pursue its own political, economic, social and cultural objectives without separation. Protecting the rights of the indigenous peoples of the world is a matter close to the heart and an important priority for Estonia in our human rights policy. This has been the case since the late 1990s, as I have just referred to in the previous question. Estonia participated in a group of countries that developed and presented the UN Declaration on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples in 2007. Estonian experts have repeatedly been members of the Permanent Forum of Indigenous Peoples; they still are. For example, Sven-Erik Soosaar is currently a member. We have consistently supported the Forum of Indigenous Peoples and the United Nations Fund for Indigenous Peoples. With the active impetus and support of Estonia, the European Union has included the rights of indigenous peoples as its priority in the UN Human Rights Forums. Estonia has over a century of expertise based on research and direct contacts about the life and situation of Finno-Ugric indigenous peoples in the Russian Federation. At international level, we have committed and consistently raised the issue of the vulnerability of these nations, the suppression of economic, social and cultural development. These were examples that the rights of indigenous peoples are on the table as a very important topic for Estonia, it has been part of Estonia's foreign policy for a long time and it will continue. The situation of the Catalans cannot be compared to the fate of the Finno-Ugric small people today. The Catalans have extensive autonomy, their cultural, educational, social and economic interests are self-targeted and their rights are protected by European values-based agreements laws. At the end of last week, the Spanish Minister of Foreign Affairs visited Estonia. In his interviews with the Estonian public, he also highlighted very clearly the fundamentals of the Catalonia Troubles and described how the Spanish Government sees this issue. These articles may be of interest to the members of the Riigikogu who are seriously interested in the Catalan question. Thank you.

The right to self-determination applies only domestically and it determines what cultural autonomy this people can have. At the same time, I am obviously not mistaken when I say that the majority of experts in international law who have dealt with the rights of indigenous peoples are of the opinion that, in a situation where one nation is democratically expressing the view that it is necessary to declare independence, for example because of forced repression, because of the state's policy, the right to self-determination includes any right to independence. The question arose whether the Minister considered the Catalans to be indigenous people at all, whether they were subject to the same principles of the Convention on the Rights of Indigenous Peoples to which Estonia has joined. And when the question came up, the comparison between the Soviet Union and the present Spain, because these answers and arguments were indeed very similar: we heard that, since Spain is a democratic rule of law, one nation has no right to independence when it is part of it. Only in the case of a non-democratic country is the right to declare independence. At the same time, this contradicts the answers to the previous inquiry. China is obviously not a democratic rule of law, but Uighurs, Tibetans, even Taiwan, do not have the right to independence. In fact, the desire to talk only about human rights through all the answers of the Minister sounded comprehensive and final, because it is a suitable subject for liberal globalist ideology, while the rights of indigenous peoples are something quite different. And when all of this is summed up, as I said when I asked the Minister: the fundamental foundation of the Estonian state, to which Estonia has been created as an independent state, is the permanent right to self-determination of the peoples, as the Manifest for the Peoples of Estonia says in 1918... More time, please!
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U378	Please, three more minutes!

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U394	Thank you, good manager. Ladies and gentlemen, We've been talking a lot about sick days, but it doesn't seem to be clear. After all, sickness benefit in itself is a very important part of the social security scheme. Its aim in Estonia has been partly to compensate workers for the loss of earnings, as people have cardiovascular diseases, colds, depression, fractures and, in a clear way, the worker must have some compensation to stay at home and be treated and then return to work. In this case, we are talking about ownership, about the employee's interest in staying healthy, about the employer's ability to design his motivation package as he pleases, and part of this motivation package can also be a generous sickness benefit. For example, if we reduce the compensation for sick days or increase the burden on employers to compensate for sick days, the social partners must agree among themselves what is right and fair. But we are not talking about whether a person with cardiovascular disease or a fracture can heal himself at home and then return to work. We have been in a crisis since last spring and, as we have seen in the emergency situation, compensation for the first three days of illness was implemented very quickly and as a very important measure, so that all people who feel sick could stay home immediately and not go into a job to spread infection in fear of income loss. Of course, we were also afraid of the disease, and 55,000 more were taken during the emergency period than in previous years. In total, compensation for the first three days of sickness cost around EUR 7.2 million. Ah, in an emergency, a total of 134000 sick lists were collected. But we're in a crisis, the threat of infection hasn't gone away. In the autumn, we discussed at length and the government finally came to a solution after four months or more of the debate, with 70% of [remuneration] being reimbursed in cooperation with employers from the second day of sickness. But we are still in a crisis, we are not talking about the short-term, perhaps one-off, need for a person to be put at risk and stay home for the sake of everyone else's health, but about the fact that the government has a limited number of instruments to control the infection. One of these instruments to reduce workplace sites, to reduce the spread of infection at workplaces, is to compensate more generously for sick days, so that people don't have to go to work in fear of income loss, thinking that maybe I don't have a corona, or that if I don't go to the test, I don't know, and maybe it just goes over and maybe it succeeds that no one gets the infection. There's no way to blame a person for hoping that he's not the one who gets the disease or who's passing on the infection. Now, in a crisis situation, we have actually come to what we read in yesterday's paper that jobs continue to be at the forefront. At the same time, the Health Board points out that some of the superiors hide close contact data, do not recommend giving up close contacts and are not directed towards self-insulation. On the one hand, the fact that a person does not want to lose income, on the other hand, clearly the employer also needs this worker. For example, you can get an infection when driving a bus to work, in recreation and smoke corners. To date, 72% of the colleges have workplaces, 61% of them are in Tallinn. Indeed, family infection is also increasing, so that some of this infection is not from workmates. As the Prime Minister said in Question Time today, 12% of the infection has come from co-workers and a third do not know where the infection came from. But whether the infection is in the family circle or somewhere else, if now an infected person, instead of staying at home with signs of disease, goes to work, travels to work with his colleagues in the morning, cooperates, takes a lunch break, then he passes the infection on to his coworkers. Now, let's review the same arguments that may have seemed completely logical in order to design the sickness benefit system before the crisis. For example, it's a liability. Understandably, for all social security schemes, there is a risk that it will be exploited, that the benefit will be received by a person who is not entitled to it. The more generous the scheme is, the greater the risk, and the more people who see that it may be useful not to follow the rules and to scheme for themselves. Of course, everyone misses pre-corona time, and so we argue with the principles of normal time. But if the criterion for compensation for the first day of illness is a positive test result or close contact identified by the Health Board, as well as close contacts included, then a person simply does not choose to stay home. Whether the sickness benefit can be less generous, we can start talking again after the spread of the contagious disease. The person is ill and must remain isolated in close contact. So this responsibility is entirely there. If the majority of the Riigikogu really thinks that this 100% of the first day is too generous and that a minimum wager still has to pay a fine of almost 100 euros for us all in two weeks' stay at home, then fine it, but we will compensate for the second day and 100% or the first day and 90%. There are opportunities to make adjustments to better meet all our expectations. But we cannot use the argument that we had in the pre-crisis period. Now, all this discussion and thinking and agreeing with the social partners. When we discussed the generous reimbursement of sick days in this very Chamber in the autumn, [we heard] both the government by the chairman of the Social Affairs Committee, and the Social Democrats suggested that [this debate is] very good and should, of course, think further, but we still need to take the time to find a solution that fits all ages and is something universal. Then they thought and discussed it for over four months. They thought, discussed, and developed a solution for four months. Temporary, as temporary as it can be. And now we're talking the same thing again about what to do with these sick days. The contribution of the social partners to changing labour legislation and social security schemes is undoubtedly a priority. We could leave much more working life for workers and employers to agree on. But we are not talking about a long-term reorganisation of the sickness compensation scheme or an increase in the burden on employers. On the contrary, we are talking about a crisis measure, about an instrument to limit the spread of infection, and about mitigating the burden of compensation for sick days for employers. Today, just as last spring, the resources for compensation for damage caused by restrictions are available in the supplementary budget. So if a company, you are in the tourism sector or somewhere else, has lost a significant part of its turnover, there is no employment for employees, then it is possible to receive crisis support. It is undoubtedly reasonable; it will help businesses survive the crisis and provide jobs for people. Those companies, for which we can say they are lucky, they can still produce food, they can still deliver goods to people at home, they have work, they do not have lost turnover, but they have the costs of personal protective equipment and restructuring work, so that people from different shifts, for example, do not come into contact with them, find a replacement if a person gets sick of finding a replacement, especially if specific skills or knowledge are needed when we are talking here, for example, about raising dairy cattle or producing bread, [it is difficult], and then they also have to pay compensation to a permanent person at home. You pay a replacement worker and compensation for a permanent person at home. It's a double load. If we knew that this crisis would last a month, [it could be said] well, hold on, it will be over. But we can't afford it. We cannot afford this to any of our employers. Rather, we will see that next year it will continue in the same way. These companies will have to bear this double burden. Now, the argument that the employer can be more generous and the local government can be more generous and pay the compensation itself from the first day of sickness. Yeah, it could be. In the event of illness, we see that jobs where people are being taken by bus in the morning, where there are large groups, jobs where work cannot be taken home, work that cannot be done on the long road. Perhaps the current compensation scheme for sick days is asymmetrical and the burden is asymmetrically borne by employers. This employer, where the risk of spreading infection is higher for workers, because workers work in large groups on the ground, there is a higher risk of infection and there is a higher risk for the employer that he can bear this burden. In contrast, it is pointed out that the second-day compensation already has an impact. That's great! It is very nice that it has been confirmed that the generous reimbursement of sick days leaves more sick people at home. This is exactly what we have been talking about so far, and similarly the survey has shown that nearly a quarter of workers, looking back at this year of crisis, say yes, they had a colleague who went to work sick, and 84% think that a more generous sickness benefit would have allowed the sick person to stay home. We are saying that Tallinn has decided to pay this compensation itself. It is excellent that a well-managed municipality decides to finance the first days of sickness. But local authorities are in a very unequal position. Government, various ministers have said that really, we are allocating funds to local governments in an additional budget, and at the expense of this, the first days of sickness can be financed. But in the same way, we hear in this Chamber that the ventilation systems in schools can be replaced at the expense of this, that it can cover other costs associated with the crisis. So this one amount of money that the municipality gets isn't enough to do all these things. If the compensation for the first day of illness is an important instrument and actually allows for a reduction of job openings, it should be national, valid in the same way for everyone, especially since, as Helmen Küttt has pointed out, this additional budget is largely financed by loan money and we will take it all together, we will pay everyone together, but the people of Tallinn will benefit now. Why such inequality? In times of crisis, inequality is amplified anyway. The role of the state should be to level it out. Then, in view of the fact that there is still the hope that we are now going to emerge from the crisis, the infection coefficient R0 is less than one, is in the range of 0.8 to 0.85, and we hope that it will stay and fall further. This means that we still have a relatively high number of infected people for several months, but according to the messages, the restrictions seem to be being relaxed. That makes sense. People are tired, no one wants the restrictions to last a day longer than necessary. But the spread of infection does not ask whether people are tired or whether we have restrictions. This means that, despite the significant mitigation of restrictions, it is necessary to motivate people to do the right thing. If we do not do so with restrictions, this can be done, for example, with a more generous sickness benefit. If, after deregulation, the government's plan is still to do everything as well as possible, to test widely, to identify the sick and close contacts, to send them home to isolation, then we can also see that not all people in close contact and, unfortunately, not all people with a positive corona test are staying at home. They either come back to work or ignore isolation rules in a different way. Instead of fine and punishing them, in the case of working people, the fact is that they do not need to lose their income is the joint contribution of all of us so that people can treat themselves calmly, stay at home, follow the rules without losing their income. And as I said in the case of employers, the burden is very unevenly distributed, the same applies to workers. People who work in larger groups cannot work from home and often earn lower than average wages. If the problem now is the high cost of sickness benefit, we will put on the ceiling that, for example, the ceiling is the average salary or median salary. Those who earn less than this will receive their full pay as sickness benefits, or those who cross this border will receive either 70% of the total or how we agree. So there are actually options. I would not write off the more generous compensation instrument for sick days if we wanted to get rid of the restrictions, because even in the autumn, as we have the spread of infectious diseases, the infection is becoming more widespread, with more colds and other diseases. In order for these sick people to stay home with the first symptoms of the disease, so that they go to the test, so that employers do not have such dual motivation or failure to give up close contacts, because it means both finding a replacement and paying sickness benefits, it would be essential for the state to bear this cost as a crisis measure. We all understand that, even in the case of deregulation, we need to take further steps to prevent the spread of the virus. What is very surprising is that there are actually no substantive arguments against it. No one says that more generous compensation for sick days does not work or is not suitable or does not help to limit the spread of infection. We also heard in Question Time today about how the government does not support the generous compensation for sick days, but mostly in a world-view. Although we are talking about a crisis measure, the argument we heard in Question Time is similarly a pre-crisis argument, where the individual's own responsibility is important, where we shape social security schemes in agreement with the social partners, where the world view is what determines decisions. But instead of defining the nature of the workplace, as we heard today in Question Time, the emphasis could still go on solving the problem that we still have so many workplace hotspots. It is therefore completely incomprehensible why the constructive proposal of the opposition aimed at curbing the spread of infection does not even recommend further discussion. Although there is no alternative plan, no one has come up with a plan to draw the line on the spreading of workplaces, how to motivate these people to stay at home, how to achieve compliance with isolation rules without any restrictions or orders or prohibitions. I hope for a meaningful debate and support for the draft tabled by the Socialists. Thank you.

ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U443	Let us move on to the sixth item, this is the first reading of the draft law amending the Health Insurance Act initiated by the Isamaa Group. I ask Urmas Reinsalu, a member of the Riigikogu, to present the speech chair of the Riigikogu.
ParlaMint-EE_2021-04-14_U444	Thank you. Honourable representative of the people! This draft law concerns an extremely important issue. This concerns the main challenge we are facing in terms of how to tackle coronavir in a more effective way. The different, faster spreading mutations of the Corona virus travel across national borders. An effective measure to prevent them is the imposition of a duty to test general persons prior to or immediately after their arrival, which is theoretically provided by the law in force. But the current practical system is built up backwards. It is usually possible to get rid of self-insulation by using two tests. In order to avoid wider restrictions on cross-border movement, it is appropriate to establish a general test principle. I would remind you that this principle has also been supported by the Scientific Advisory Board in several of its presentations to the Government of the Republic this year. Why hasn't this been done before? According to officials, the failure to implement such a system in the past has been due to the fact that the Health Insurance Act provides for the implementation of a quarantine provided by sickness benefit in the event of refusal to diagnose. However, in the case of an existing reverse self-insulation system, no sickness benefit is paid for being self-insulated. Enabling the system proposed in the draft will place particular emphasis on the introduction of general testing in cross-border movements and will provide a legal opportunity to seek harmonisation of rules, particularly with the countries of our region. I would like to remind you that the general principle of testing to prevent the British strain was introduced at the beginning of this year in essence by the majority of European countries. We can talk here about a great many of the Member States of the European Union. It is also legally necessary to make testing a primary requirement and quarantine an alternative to refusing it. Right now, the situation is backwards. In short, what does this bill allow? This draft law removes the technical obstacles that the government is now claiming to have to face in the absence of universal border testing by the government. Now, here we have been talking, and the Prime Minister has also referred to 'fundamental arguments' as to why it is not possible to test at the overall borders, as if it were contrary to human rights and fundamental rights. I am sure that the initiators of the draft do not agree with this approach. This is proportionate, namely, to prevent cross-border transmission of the virus in a situation where we have already seen and paid an expensive price for the mass spread of the British strain across national borders to Estonia. I believe that when we talk about returning to normal free movement, it is certainly reasonable for us to cooperate with the countries in our region in such a way that similar measures have been implemented, as other countries in our region do. That is why this vote is essentially about this bill. Do we think that a measure to control the corona virus should be implemented, as proposed by the Scientific Advisory Board, which is used by most European countries and which has been considered proportionate and appropriate in order to avoid a general ban on cross-border movements, as well as the European Commission's recommendations, providing for either testing or quarantine? According to the model we have proposed, this is an opportunity for the government, but we must certainly first of all create this legal basis so that there is no inappropriate implementation here. Secondly, I would like to stress that we actually had a talk about this in the old government, if Jüri remembers. And then there were officials who said it couldn't be done because the law was legally flawed. I asked when the Social Security officials would work it out. They said they're working on it. We cannot stall, Parliament must come to the aid of the government if this legal framework has not been achieved so far. And who comes to the aid of this other than the benevolent Fatherland Party, in order to close this gap so that we can protect Estonia, the Estonian people in the most efficient and proportionate way from the cross-border spread of the corona virus, precisely in the conditions of spread of mutated virus strains. So I'm asking the National Council to support this bill. It is a draft law drawn up in a spirit of cooperation between political parties, which is written out of only one clear objective: that we are able to rule out this virus without fail. Thank you.
